<meta name="description" content="Williams syndrome (WS) is a relatively rare microdeletion disorder that occurs in as many as 1:7,500 individuals. It arises due to mispairing of low-copy DNA repetitive elements at meiosis. Deletion size is similar across most individuals with WS ...">

<hgroup><h1>Williams syndrome</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Kozel%20BA"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Beth A Kozel</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">

<div id="aip_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none">
<div class="p" id="A1">

<div class="author-notes p"><div class="fn" id="FN1">
<p id="P1">Author contributions</p>
<p id="P2">B.A.K. and B.R.P. developed the outline for the manuscript, engaged the contributors and synthesized the final manuscript. All authors participated in the research, writing, and editing of the document, and all approved the final version of the manuscript. C.K. provided clinical photos and M.P. conducted the family experience interview.</p>
</div></div>
</div>
<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Collection date 2021 Jun 17.</p></section></div></div>
<div id="clp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="p"><a href="/about/copyright/" class="usa-link">PMC Copyright notice</a></div></div>
</div>
<div>PMCID: PMC9437774  NIHMSID: NIHMS1827175  PMID: <a href="https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/34140529/" class="usa-link">34140529</a>
</div>
<div class="ra xbox p" role="complementary" aria-label="Related or updated information about this article"><div>The publisher's version of this article is available at <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Nat Rev Dis Primers</a>
</div></div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="ABS1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p id="P3">Williams syndrome (WS) is a relatively rare microdeletion disorder that occurs in as many as 1:7,500 individuals. It arises due to mispairing of low-copy DNA repetitive elements at meiosis. Deletion size is similar across most individuals with WS and leads to loss of one copy of 25–27 genes on chromosome 7q11.23. The resulting unique disorder affects multiple systems, with cardinal features including, but not limited to, cardiovascular disease (characteristically stenosis of the great arteries and most notably supravalvar aortic stenosis), a distinctive craniofacial appearance and a specific cognitive and behavioural profile that includes intellectual disability and hypersociability. Genotype–phenotype evidence is strongest for the elastin (<em>ELN</em>) gene, which is responsible for the vascular and connective tissue features of WS, and for the transcription factor genes <em>GTF2I</em> and <em>GTF2IRD1</em>, which are known to affect intellectual ability, social functioning and anxiety. Mounting evidence also ascribes phenotypic consequences to deletion of <em>BAZ1B, LIMK1, STX1A and MLXIPL</em>, but more work is needed to understand the mechanism by which these deletions contribute to clinical outcomes. Age of diagnosis has fallen in regions of the world where technological advances, such as chromosomal microarray, enable clinicians to make the diagnosis of WS without formally suspecting it, allowing earlier intervention by medical and developmental specialists. Phenotypic variability is considerable for all cardinal features of WS, but the specific sources of this variability remain unknown. Further investigation to identify factors responsible for these differences may lead to mechanism-based rather than symptom-based therapies and, therefore, should be a high research priority.</p></section><section id="S1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p id="P4">Williams syndrome (WS; also known as Williams–Beuren syndrome; OMIM 194050), is a distinctive multisystem disorder (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>, <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplementary box 1</a>). The most common areas of involvement include the cardiovascular, central nervous, gastrointestinal and endocrine systems, although any organ system could be affected. Recognition of WS as a distinct clinical syndrome dates back to the mid-20<sup>th</sup> century<sup><a href="#R1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R1">1</a>–<a href="#R3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R3">3</a></sup>, and knowledge of the phenotype has steadily expanded over the ensuing ~60 years.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F1"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1. Salient features of Williams syndrome.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6029/9437774/b38b98d0ef92/nihms-1827175-f0001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1255" width="1800" alt="Figure 1."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P148">The age of peak prevalence and frequency of prominent signs or symptoms in organ systems affected in Williams syndrome are indicated. Features in bold are the common presenting features listed in the <a href="#S14" class="usa-link">Clinical Diagnosis</a> sectionin the main text. Further reading for features in plain text is included in <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplementary box 1</a>.</p>
<p id="P149"><sup>a</sup>Estimates of co-occurring autism spectrum disorder (ASD) vary (12–20%). Most individuals with Williams syndrome who have ASD fit in Wing and Gould’s active-but-odd subtype of ASD<sup><a href="#R292" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R292">292</a></sup> rather than the aloof subtype of ASD<sup><a href="#R232" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R232">232</a>,<a href="#R292" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R292">292</a></sup>. As such, the diagnosis of, and interventions for, ASD in Williams syndrome are complex and ideally benefit from engagement of practitioners who are knowledgeable about both disorders. <sup>b</sup>Sound sensitivities include one or more of the following: hyperacusis, odynacusis, auditory allodynia and auditory fascinations. ADHD, attention deficit hyperactivity disorder; CV, cardiovascular; DM, diabetes mellitus; GERD, gastro-oesophageal reflux disease; ID, intellectual disability.</p>
<p id="P150">Note: <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a> was re-drawn by the Nature Reviews Disease Primers art editor and permission for reproduction of the final figure was not granted by the publisher. Please see the published version of the paper for the final <a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Fig. 1</a> at DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a></p></figcaption></figure><p id="P5">We now know that cardiovascular disease is present in most individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">4</a>–<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">6</a></sup>, with the most frequent abnormalities involving vascular stenoses of medium- and large-sized arteries (referred to as elastin arteriopathy)<sup><a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">7</a></sup>. Depending on the location, severity and timing of onset, the management of the vasculopathy consists of noninvasive or surgical intervention complemented by life-long monitoring. Additional cardiovascular features include hypertension and a small but increased risk of cardiovascular sudden death<sup><a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">8</a>–<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a></sup>. The neurodevelopmental phenotype is unique and multifaceted. Mild to moderate intellectual disability is common but not universal and is seen in conjunction with a distinct cognitive profile of relative strengths and weaknesses<sup><a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>,<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a></sup>. In addition, there is a characteristic personality profile that includes overfriendliness, shortened attention span and/or distractibility, nonsocial specific phobias and anxiety<sup><a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">15</a>,<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">16</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P6">The genetic basis of WS was first identified in 1993, when fluorescence <em>in situ</em> hybridization (FISH) studies showed deletion of an elastin allele (<em>ELN</em>) on chromosome 7q<sup><a href="#R17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R17">17</a></sup>. We now know that WS is caused by a &lt;2-million base pair (Mb) microdeletion on chromosome 7q11.23 and that the local genomic architecture predisposes to <em>de novo</em> occurrence of this deletion<sup><a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">18</a></sup>. Individuals with WS are, therefore, hemizygous for the 25–27 genes that map to this interval, and reduction in the gene product of several key genes contributes to specific aspects of the WS phenotype.</p>
<p id="P7">Since WS was initially described, advances have been made in our understanding of the complexity and changing nature of the phenotype, the genetic basis of WS, the mechanisms that lead to selected phenotypes and the benefit of certain interventions. Yet, many more unanswered questions remain. Accordingly, our ability to optimize care and improve outcomes is modest.</p>
<p id="P8">In this Primer, we provide a snapshot of selected WS features across the lifespan, outline current and emerging diagnostic technologies, discuss the genetic basis of some of the most impactful WS features, and elaborate on pathophysiological mechanisms, where known. This information is key for formulating high-priority future research questions, the answers to which could accelerate WS-specific treatments.</p></section><section id="S2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Epidemiology</h2>
<p id="P9">WS is a rare panethnic genetic condition. Although this syndrome has been described in different populations around the world, most reports address clinical and molecular findings, with little focus on epidemiological data. The most widely cited epidemiological study is from Norway<sup><a href="#R19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R19">19</a></sup>, which reports a prevalence of 1 in 7,500 live births, a prevalence which is higher than that often cited in many non-epidemiologic sources<sup><a href="#R20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R20">20</a>,<a href="#R21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R21">21</a></sup>. WS is sufficiently rare that it is unfamiliar to most doctors, scientists, and researchers.</p>
<p id="P10">The age of diagnosis of WS has trended towards younger ages over the past decades, most notably in high-income countries with greater availability of molecular diagnostic testing. In cohorts from the USA and Australia, the median age of diagnosis decreased by more than 2 years to around 1 year of age since the 1980s (B.A.K. and M.P., unpublished work). (<a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a> and <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplementary box 2</a> were removed in post-acceptance processing). However, series from other countries indicate that diagnosis is often still established during childhood rather than infancy, even with access to molecular confirmation<sup><a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">22</a>,<a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">23</a></sup>. Several studies reported particular difficulty in diagnosing WS in African populations (or in those of African descent)<sup><a href="#R24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R24">24</a>–<a href="#R26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R26">26</a></sup>, owing to a variety of factors. Unfortunately, nearly all studies on WS, independent of topic, describe Caucasian individuals, and few studies whose participants represent diverse populations have been published<sup><a href="#R27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R27">27</a></sup>. Of note, the absence of clinically evident cardiovascular disease was associated with later diagnosis<sup><a href="#R28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R28">28</a></sup>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F2"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 2:</h3>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P151">Note: Although <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a> (as cited in this text) was accepted after peer review, it was removed from the final manuscript at production. This figure does not exist in the final paper at DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a></p></figcaption></figure><p id="P11">The prevalence of WS is comparable in males and females<sup><a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>,<a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a></sup>. However, males are more likely to have severe cardiac disease<sup><a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">30</a></sup>, especially supravalvar aortic stenosis (SVAS)<sup><a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a>,<a href="#R31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R31">31</a></sup>. There is no evidence that WS prevalence changes with parental age. Moreover, consistent differences in phenotype have been associated with the sex of the transmitting parent (that is, whether the deletion arose in the sperm or the egg)<sup><a href="#R18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R18">18</a>,<a href="#R32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R32">32</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P12">Vascular anomalies, such as SVAS and stenosis of other large arteries including the pulmonary arteries, descending aorta, renals, mesenterics, and coronary arteries contribute to morbidity and mortality across the life span<sup><a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">4</a></sup>. Other features, such as diverticular disease and aortic or mitral valve dysfunction, may also influence mortality in older age groups but are to-date insufficiently quantified in the literature. Sudden death incidence has been reported as 1:1000 patient years and is often associated with administration of sedation or anaesthesia for cardiac surgery; this rate is 25 to 100 times higher than in the age-matched general population<sup><a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a></sup>. Several adult cohorts have been described with participants as old as 86, but few studies included long-term adult follow up<sup><a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">33</a>,<a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">34</a></sup>, making it difficult to accurately estimate the life expectancy of individuals with WS.</p></section><section id="S3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Mechanisms/pathophysiology</h2>
<section id="S4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">General mechanisms underlying WS</h3>
<p id="P13">In the 1990s, compelling evidence emerged indicating that WS is a genetic disorder with an autosomal dominant mode of inheritance<sup><a href="#R35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R35">35</a>–<a href="#R38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R38">38</a></sup>. Non-genetic risk factors are not known to contribute to the occurrence of WS.</p>
<section id="S5"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Genomic structure and rearrangements.</h4>
<p id="P14">WS is caused by the pathological loss of the Williams syndrome critical region (WSCR), a 1.55–1.83 Mb region which encompasses 25–27 unique protein-coding genes on chromosome 7q11.23. The WSCR frequently undergoes rearrangement due to the presence of large, complex segmental duplications termed low-copy repeats (LCRs), which are highly similar to one another and flank the WSCR<sup><a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">39</a></sup>. The LCRs extend for several hundreds of kilobases, are comprised of genes and pseudogenes organized into distinct blocks (designated A, B and C), and contain extensive stretches of &gt;99% nucleotide identity. They are thought to have emerged during primate evolution — first by duplication of smaller segments and later by transposable element (for example, <em>Alu</em>-mediated) shuffling, to produce the complex arrangement that exists in humans today<sup><a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">40</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P15">The LCRs mediate non-allelic homologous recombination (NAHR) events between the highly similar DNA sequences during meiosis, resulting in an increased rate of <em>de novo</em> copy number variation (CNV) events within the region<sup><a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">39</a>,<a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">41</a>–<a href="#R43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R43">43</a></sup> (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). The WS deletion commonly occurs through NAHR between B block sequences in direct orientation with respect to each other, with the specific breakpoints depend on the precise site of NAHR<sup><a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">39</a></sup>. The reciprocal event (that is, duplication of the same genomic area) produces a condition referred to as 7q11.23 duplication syndrome, resulting in 3 copies of each WSCR gene. Recombination between B blocks (LCRs with the highest nucleotide identity) in an inverted, rather than direct, orientation, results in an inversion of the intervening chromosome segment<sup><a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">42</a></sup> (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). This inversion, which is present in 6–7% of the general population<sup><a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">44</a></sup>, does not cause symptoms<sup><a href="#R45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R45">45</a></sup> but seems to increase the incidence of subsequent meiotic rearrangements<sup><a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">42</a>,<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">44</a></sup>. Other LCR-specific rearrangements have also been observed at a higher frequency in the transmitting parents of children with WS<sup><a href="#R41" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R41">41</a></sup>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F3"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 3. Genomic organization of the Williams syndrome critical region.</h5>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P152">The vast majority of Williams syndrome (WS) deletions (~90%) span a 1.55-million base pair (Mb) region encompassing 25–27 protein-coding genes in the Williams syndrome critical region (WSCR) on chromosome 7q11.23, with the remainder being slightly larger (1.83 Mb)<sup><a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">39</a></sup>. The larger, less common deletion occurs between low-copy repeats (LCRs) designated as ‘A blocks’, which are present in the great apes, whereas the smaller, more common deletion occurs between ‘B blocks’, which are only present in humans. B blocks originate from a more recent evolutionary duplication event and have a higher sequence identity than A blocks<sup><a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">40</a></sup>. The WS deletion commonly occurs through non-allelic homologous recombination between B block sequences in direct orientation with respect to each other<sup><a href="#R39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R39">39</a></sup>; however, recombination between B blocks in an inverted orientation also occurs, resulting in inversion of the intervening chromosome segment<sup><a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">42</a></sup>. Smaller, atypical deletions occur infrequently but can provide valuable insight into the genotype–phenotype relationship<sup><a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">59</a>,<a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">62</a>,<a href="#R138" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R138">138</a>–<a href="#R140" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R140">140</a></sup>. Several names exist for many of the genes mapping to WSCR (see <sup><a href="#R293" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R293">293</a></sup>and <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/gene/" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/gene/</a>). ID, intellectual disability.</p>
<p id="P153">Note: <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a> was drawn by the Nature Reviews Disease Primers art editor and permission for reproduction of the final figure was not granted by the publisher. This material is labeled as <a href="#F2" class="usa-link">Fig. 2</a> in the published version of the paper DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a></p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="S6"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Atypical deletions.</h4>
<p id="P16">While most deletions span the typical 1.55–1.83 Mb interval at 7q11.23, there are individuals with rare deletions that encompass smaller or larger segments of the WSCR, often with one common and one unique breakpoint. Larger deletions that extend beyond the WSCR generally cause additional features. When these deletions extend toward the telomere and span the <em>YWHAG</em> and/or <em>MAGI2</em> genes, seizures are common<sup><a href="#R46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R46">46</a>,<a href="#R47" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R47">47</a></sup>, although there are reports of epilepsy in individuals with the typical deletion<sup><a href="#R48" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R48">48</a></sup>. Inclusion of the <em>AUTS2</em> gene on the centromeric side may result in smaller head size than is usually seen in WS<sup><a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">49</a></sup>, and larger deletions can also alter the characteristic WS behavioural profile<sup><a href="#R49" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R49">49</a>,<a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a></sup>. Deletion of <em>HIP1</em> (formerly <em>HSP27</em>) in particular has been associated with more severe intellectual disability<sup><a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a>,<a href="#R51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R51">51</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P17">Smaller deletions result in a subset of the phenotypic features seen in classic WS<sup><a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">40</a>,<a href="#R50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R50">50</a>,<a href="#R52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R52">52</a>–<a href="#R64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R64">64</a></sup>, but clear-cut correlations between deletion size and specific phenotypic features are challenging, with the exception of <em>ELN</em>. This is likely due to the paucity and variety of small deletions, differing methods for phenotypic assessment, and high likelihood of the combinatorial effects of gene deletion. Several specific genotype–phenotype relationships are discussed in the next section.</p></section><section id="S7"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Genomic analyses.</h4>
<p id="P18">CNV events of the 7q11.23 region have been shown to affect both gene transcription and DNA methylation across the entire genome. Initial studies in WS lymphoblast cell lines identified dysregulation of genes involved in glycolysis and neuronal migration<sup><a href="#R65" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R65">65</a></sup>, while subsequent studies of blood RNA highlighted upregulation of three gene expression modules linked to B cell activation, RNA processing and RNA transport<sup><a href="#R66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R66">66</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P19">Transcriptome analysis of more relevant cell types has been made possible by the ability to reprogramme somatic cells into induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSCs) and direct them down specific cell lineages<sup><a href="#R67" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R67">67</a></sup>. For example, iPSC-derived cortical neurons from individuals with WS show reduced expression of genes involved in neurotransmitter receptor activity, synaptic assembly and potassium channel complexes<sup><a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">68</a></sup>. Comparison of gene expression in WS iPSCs with those from individuals with 7q11.23 duplication syndrome revealed that many of the differentially expressed genes have a symmetrically opposite pattern of expression<sup><a href="#R69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R69">69</a></sup>. A similar symmetrical gene–dose-dependent pattern was seen in DNA methylation analysis of blood DNA from individuals with WS (7q11.23 deletion) and those with the 7q11.23 duplication syndrome<sup><a href="#R70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R70">70</a></sup>, suggesting that CNV in this area affects epigenetic regulation of the genome.</p></section></section><section id="S8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Molecular mechanisms</h3>
<p id="P20">The WSCR contains 25–27 genes and several noncoding RNAs. Knowledge about how each of these genes contributes to the WS phenotype is still growing (<a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>). Several mouse models inform those efforts, including single gene knockouts, as well as deletion of the entire WSCR (CD)<sup><a href="#R71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R71">71</a>–<a href="#R73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R73">73</a></sup> and two half-deletions (PD and DD)<sup><a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">74</a></sup>. To refine genotype-phenotype correlations, we will focus only on selected single gene knockout models.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4. Phenotypic consequences of deleting key genes in the Williams syndrome critical region.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6029/9437774/94c2409c4979/nihms-1827175-f0002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1271" width="1800" alt="Figure 4."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P154">Putative or elucidated genotype–phenotype relationships for six genes in the Williams syndrome critical region (WSCR) are depicted, including elastin (ELN), general transcription factor II-I (GTF2I), BAZ1B, LIM domain kinase 1 (LIMK1), syntaxin 1A (STX1A) and carbohydrate-responsive element-binding protein (ChREBP). Phenotypes in mouse models and in individuals with Williams syndrome are indicated for <em>ELN</em> and the <em>GTF2I</em> genes because, presently, their mechanisms of action are best delineated and they offer the best targets for therapy.</p>
<p id="P155">Note: <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a> was redrawn by the Nature Reviews Disease Primers art editor and permission for reproduction of the final figure was not granted by the publisher. This version was designed using BioRender. This material is labeled as <a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Fig. 3</a> in the published version of the paper DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a></p></figcaption></figure><p id="P21">Seven genes (<em>BAZ1B</em>, <em>VPS37D</em>, <em>STX1A</em>, <em>LIMK1</em>, <em>CLIP2</em>, <em>GTF2IRD1</em> and <em>GTF2I</em>) have a probability of loss-of-function intolerance (pLI) score of 0.9 or higher<sup><a href="#R75" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R75">75</a></sup>, suggesting that only a subset of the genes in the WSCR directly contributes to phenotype. A pLI score is calculated by examining the frequency of loss of function variants in a population; fewer variants than expected is associated with a higher score and implies a greater likelihood of pathogenicity. It is important to point out that the gene with the greatest support for a role in the phenotypic consequences of WS is <em>ELN</em>, which has a pLI of 0. Similarly, considerable evidence implicates deletion of <em>MLXIPL</em> (pLI = 0.05) in metabolic aspects of WS. These two examples highlight the inadequacy of predictors such as pLI in identifying all pathogenetic genes. An overview of the best characterized genotype–phenotype correlations for WSCR genes is provided below.</p>
<section id="S9"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Elastin.</h4>
<p id="P22">The <em>ELN</em> gene is transcribed in tissues that stretch and recoil, such as the lungs, skin and elastic arteries (including the aorta, where elastin accounts for up to 50% of the vessel’s dry weight)<sup><a href="#R76" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R76">76</a>,<a href="#R77" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R77">77</a></sup>. The protein consists of repeating hydrophobic and crosslinking domains. The crosslinks allow monomers to be bound to one another in a highly interwoven polymer that allows for distribution of force, while the hydrophobic domains drive the recoil process through entropy when they are exposed to an aqueous environment with tissue expansion (stretch)<sup><a href="#R78" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R78">78</a>,<a href="#R79" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R79">79</a></sup>. The polymer is long-lived, with a short window for deposition and a calculated half-life of 74 years<sup><a href="#R80" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R80">80</a></sup>. Interestingly, although robust elastogenesis occurs only during early growth and development, <em>ELN</em> is thought to be continually transcribed throughout the lifespan<sup><a href="#R81" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R81">81</a></sup>. Outside of this tight developmental window, <em>ELN</em> transcripts are quickly turned over<sup><a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">82</a>,<a href="#R83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R83">83</a></sup>. As such, this connection between transcription, translation and assembly is ripe for investigation.</p>
<p id="P23">Individuals with loss-of-function point mutations<sup><a href="#R84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R84">84</a>–<a href="#R86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R86">86</a></sup> or intragenic deletions<sup><a href="#R87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R87">87</a></sup> within <em>ELN</em> have ELN-associated familial SVAS, and develop cardiovascular manifestations that are indistinguishable from those found in WS. Common features include focal or long-segment stenosis (narrowing) of the large elastic arteries in the setting of a globally narrow and thick-walled vasculature<sup><a href="#R4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R4">4</a>,<a href="#R6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R6">6</a>,<a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">7</a>,<a href="#R30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R30">30</a>,<a href="#R88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R88">88</a></sup>. Stenosis of the supravalvar aortic and supravalvar pulmonary arteries are the most common and show considerable variability in severity<sup><a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a>,<a href="#R89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R89">89</a></sup>. While pulmonary artery stenoses often improves with age, narrowing on the aortic side may stay the same, improve or worsen with time <sup><a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>,<a href="#R88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R88">88</a>,<a href="#R90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R90">90</a></sup>. Other vessels, such as the descending aorta, renal arteries, mesenteric arteries and coronaries may also exhibit stenosis, with symptomatology pointing to hypoperfusion of the associated end organ (hypertension, abdominal pain, and cardiac hypoperfusion with ST elevation or sudden death). Even in the absence of stenosis, individuals with <span class="text-underline">either</span> WS or familial SVAS have high rates of hypertension and vascular stiffness, which are detectable as early as infancy and childhood<sup><a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">8</a>,<a href="#R91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R91">91</a></sup>. An increased sudden death relative risk with and without anaesthesia has been reported<sup><a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">10</a>,<a href="#R92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R92">92</a>–<a href="#R94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R94">94</a></sup>; the precise mechanism by which this occurs is not currently known but it is expected to be multifactorial, reflecting the complex vascular pathophysiology<sup><a href="#R95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R95">95</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P24">Heterozygous <em>Eln</em> knockout (<em>Eln</em><sup>+/–</sup>) mice recapitulate relevant cardiovascular features of WS, including aortic wall thickening, hypertension and cardiac hypertrophy<sup><a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">7</a></sup>,however, it has been difficult to replicate the hourglass-type supravalvar stenoses commonly seen in individuals with WS or familial SVAS. Neither <em>Eln</em><sup>+/–</sup>nor transgenic <em>Eln</em><sup>–/–</sup> mice expressing the human <em>ELN</em> gene (<em>Eln</em><sup>–/–</sup>;<em>ELN</em><sup>+</sup> mice, which have 30% of normal elastin levels) develop frank stenosis, although <em>Eln</em><sup>–/–</sup>; <em>ELN</em><sup>+</sup> mice exhibit more severe arterial wall thickening, luminal narrowing, hypertension and cardiac hypertrophy than <em>Eln</em><sup>+/–</sup> mice<sup><a href="#R96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R96">96</a></sup>. Discrete stenosis or coarctation (congenital narrowing) of the aortic arch as well as the development of neointima (thickening of the intima characteristic of segmental aortic stenoses in WS) has been described in mice with a homozygous <em>Eln</em> deletion restricted to vascular smooth muscle cells<sup><a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">97</a></sup>. Unfortunately, most of these mice do not survive past postnatal day 18.</p>
<p id="P25">Several hypotheses have been postulated for the mechanism by which elastin insufficiency causes large vessel arteriopathy. Segmental stenoses are thought to develop through increased proliferation and migration of vascular smooth muscle cells due to a reduction or lack of elastin<sup><a href="#R98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R98">98</a>–<a href="#R100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R100">100</a></sup>. Lineage tracing indicates that the excess smooth muscle cells responsible for inward remodeling of the arterial wall are not clonal but are derived from multiple existing smooth muscle cells in the media layer<sup><a href="#R101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R101">101</a></sup>. Inward remodeling is caused in part by excess integrin β3 signaling, as genetic or pharmacological inhibition of this pathway reduces vascular pathology and extends lifespan in <em>Eln</em><sup>–/–</sup> mice<sup><a href="#R101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R101">101</a></sup>. Other work suggests that rather than an increase in proliferation, elastin insufficiency produces medial fibrosis, altered mobility of smooth muscle cells and abnormal circumferential growth, leading to smaller lumen size and thicker arterial walls<sup><a href="#R102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R102">102</a></sup>. Additional studies suggest that hypertension arises in <em>Eln</em><sup>+/–</sup> mice as part of a developmental adaptation to normalize vessel wall stress, capitalizing on the increased pressures to prop open the narrow, stiff elastin-insufficient vessels<sup><a href="#R103" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R103">103</a></sup>, although more recent studies indicate that reactive oxygen species (ROS) production may also have a role<sup><a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">74</a></sup>. Several additional molecular and cellular mechanisms impact the pathogenesis of elastin arteriopathy, including mechanistic target of rapamycin (mTOR) perturbation of smooth muscle mechanosensing <sup><a href="#R104" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R104">104</a>–<a href="#R106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R106">106</a></sup>, and the adaptive immune system<sup><a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P26">In addition to vascular disease, patients (and mice) with elastin insufficiency have impaired pulmonary<sup><a href="#R107" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R107">107</a>–<a href="#R109" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R109">109</a></sup> and skin<sup><a href="#R110" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R110">110</a>,<a href="#R111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R111">111</a></sup> elastic fibres, leading to impaired tissue mechanics. Other common connective tissue features of WS may also be linked to elastin insufficiency, such as periumbilical or inguinal hernias<sup><a href="#R112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R112">112</a></sup>, hoarse voice<sup><a href="#R113" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R113">113</a></sup>, earlier-onset skin wrinkling<sup><a href="#R111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R111">111</a></sup>, atypical scar formation<sup><a href="#R111" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R111">111</a></sup> and genitourinary phenotypes<sup><a href="#R112" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R112">112</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="S10"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>NCF1</em> modification of elastin-mediated hypertension.</h4>
<p id="P27">Neutrophil cytosolic factor 1 (<em>NCF1</em>) resides at the telomeric end of the WSCR. Two <em>NCF1</em> pseudogenes, <em>NCF1B</em> and <em>NCF1C</em>, are present in the LCR regions that flank the typical deletion. <em>NCF1</em> is the regulatory subunit for several NADPH oxidase (NOX) complexes and generates ROS in multiple cell types, including endothelial cells, smooth muscle cells and leukocytes, following various stresses<sup><a href="#R114" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R114">114</a>,<a href="#R115" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R115">115</a></sup>. A deletion that removes <em>NCF1</em> is found in ~50% of individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R91">91</a>,<a href="#R116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R116">116</a></sup>. Loss of <em>NCF1</em> has been associated with relative protection from hypertension and vascular stiffness in individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R91">91</a>,<a href="#R116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R116">116</a></sup> and in animal models<sup><a href="#R74" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R74">74</a>,<a href="#R117" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R117">117</a>,<a href="#R118" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R118">118</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="S11"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">
<em>GTF2I</em> and <em>GTF2IRD1.</em>
</h4>
<p id="P28">General transcription factor 2-I (<em>GTF2I</em>) and GTF2I repeat domain-containing 1 (<em>GTF2IRD1</em>) are paralogous genes located on adjacent loci at the telomeric end of the WSCR. They encode transcription factors and contribute to typical WS behaviour and development. On the molecular level, <em>GTF2IRD1</em> and <em>GTF2I</em> encode BEN and GTFII-I, respectively, which are members of a versatile protein family with broad functional activities<sup><a href="#R119" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R119">119</a>–<a href="#R121" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R121">121</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P29">GTFII-I is a highly conserved and ubiquitously expressed multifunctional transcription factor<sup><a href="#R122" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R122">122</a>–<a href="#R125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R125">125</a></sup> that regulates gene expression<sup><a href="#R120" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R120">120</a>,<a href="#R126" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R126">126</a>,<a href="#R127" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R127">127</a></sup> through interactions with tissue-specific transcription factors and complexes related to chromatin remodelling<sup><a href="#R125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R125">125</a></sup>. GTFII-I is activated in response to various extracellular signals and then translocates to the nucleus<sup><a href="#R121" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R121">121</a>,<a href="#R125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R125">125</a>,<a href="#R126" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R126">126</a>,<a href="#R128" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R128">128</a></sup>. GTFII-I has been shown to be involved in multiple processes, which include regulating embryonic development<sup><a href="#R122" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R122">122</a>,<a href="#R129" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R129">129</a>,<a href="#R130" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R130">130</a></sup>, the cell cycle<sup><a href="#R125" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R125">125</a>,<a href="#R127" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R127">127</a>,<a href="#R131" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R131">131</a>,<a href="#R132" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R132">132</a></sup>, actin cytoskeleton dynamics, axon guidance<sup><a href="#R132" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R132">132</a></sup> and epigenetic regulation<sup><a href="#R133" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R133">133</a>,<a href="#R134" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R134">134</a></sup>. Indeed, an iPSC-based study showed that <em>GTF2I</em> alterations are responsible for 10–20% of the transcription dysregulation in disease-relevant pathways in WS and in the 7q11.23 duplication syndrome, beginning in the pluripotent state and further amplified during development<sup><a href="#R69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R69">69</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P30">From a phenotypic standpoint, individuals with classic deletions of the WSCR<sup><a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a></sup> and those with shorter deletions that result in loss of <em>GTF2IRD1</em> and <em>GTF2I</em><sup><a href="#R62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R62">62</a></sup> (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>) typically show intellectual disability, high social approach to familiar persons and indiscriminate social approach to strangers (also referred to as social disinhibition or hypersociability), and difficulties in social communication (pragmatics). By contrast, individuals with deletions that spare these two genes typically exhibit neither intellectual disability<sup><a href="#R40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R40">40</a>,<a href="#R54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R54">54</a>,<a href="#R56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R56">56</a>,<a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">59</a>,<a href="#R135" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R135">135</a>–<a href="#R137" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R137">137</a></sup> nor these social characteristics<sup><a href="#R59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R59">59</a>,<a href="#R138" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R138">138</a></sup>. Interestingly, individuals with shorter WS deletions that spare <em>GTF2I</em> but remove <em>GTF2IRD1</em> also typically do not exhibit intellectual disability or hypersociability<sup><a href="#R136" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R136">136</a>,<a href="#R139" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R139">139</a></sup>, but they do show increased social approach to familiar people and difficulty with social pragmatics<sup><a href="#R136" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R136">136</a></sup>. Further evidence for the role of <em>GTF2I</em> gene dose in intellectual ability comes from a family with a very short duplication affecting <em>GTF2I</em> but not <em>GTF2IRD1</em>; all family members with <em>GTF2I</em> duplication had intellectual disability, whereas the intellectual ability of those with the usual two copies of <em>GTF2I</em> was average<sup><a href="#R140" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R140">140</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P31">As in humans, partial hemizygous deletion of the mouse WSCR including <em>Gtf2i</em> results in increased sociability but also impairs motor coordination in mutant mice<sup><a href="#R71" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R71">71</a></sup>. Homozygous deletion of <em>Gtf2i</em> in mice results in embryonic lethality owing to severe developmental abnormalities<sup><a href="#R122" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R122">122</a>,<a href="#R124" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R124">124</a>,<a href="#R141" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R141">141</a></sup>, such as exencephaly and neural tube disclosure, whereas heterozygous mice show impaired social habituation to an unfamiliar mouse<sup><a href="#R141" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R141">141</a>,<a href="#R142" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R142">142</a></sup>. Selective deletion of <em>Gtf2i</em> in excitatory neurons leads to myelination alterations, motor deficits and hypersociability, which normalize with pharmacological rescue of myelination<sup><a href="#R143" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R143">143</a>,<a href="#R144" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R144">144</a></sup>. Intracisternal <em>Gtf2i</em>-gene therapy in the CD (full WS deletion) mouse model resulted in beneficial effects on behavioral deficits related to motor, social and anxiety-like behaviors<sup><a href="#R145" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R145">145</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P32">Taken together, these findings suggest that loss of a <em>GTF2I</em> allele is a major contributor to the intellectual disability and social disinhibition that are characteristics of individuals with WS. Deletion of <em>GTF2IRD1</em>, even without deletion of <em>GTF2I</em>, likely contributes to the social communication difficulties and generally increased social approach. Findings of dose-dependent <em>Gtf2i</em>-specific social and anxiety phenotypes in mouse models<sup><a href="#R146" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R146">146</a>,<a href="#R147" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R147">147</a></sup> converge with those observed in the human hemideletion and duplication syndromes<sup><a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>,<a href="#R146" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R146">146</a>,<a href="#R148" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R148">148</a>,<a href="#R149" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R149">149</a></sup>. In a study of the effects of <em>Gtf2i</em> copy number on cortical neuron maturation and function, mice with a single copy of <em>Gtf2i</em> showed increased axonal outgrowth, whereas this outgrowth was decreased in mice with three <em>Gtf2i</em> copies<sup><a href="#R150" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R150">150</a></sup>. The axonal growth effects of GTFII-I might occur through regulating the expression of the homeobox proteins DLX5 and DLX6<sup><a href="#R151" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R151">151</a></sup>, thereby affecting the excitatory/inhibitory balance in the brain<sup><a href="#R152" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R152">152</a></sup>. This balance has been suggested as a possible mechanism that causes autism spectrum disorder (ASD)<sup><a href="#R153" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R153">153</a></sup>. In this regard, it is noteworthy that both deletion<sup><a href="#R154" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R154">154</a>,<a href="#R155" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R155">155</a></sup> and duplication<sup><a href="#R156" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R156">156</a></sup> of the WSCR are associated with elevated rates of ASD.</p></section><section id="S12"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Other candidate genes.</h4>
<p id="P33">For several other genes within the WSCR, there is emerging evidence of an association with components of the Williams syndrome phenotype. Bromodomain adjacent to zinc finger domain 1B (<em>BAZ1B</em>), a member of the B-WICH chromatin remodelling complex, is essential for correct neural crest cell migration <em>in vitro</em><sup><a href="#R157" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R157">157</a></sup> and <em>in vivo</em><sup><a href="#R158" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R158">158</a></sup> and has been proposed as a master regulator of human craniofacial development<sup><a href="#R157" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R157">157</a></sup>. As the enteric nervous system is also derived from the neural crest<sup><a href="#R159" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R159">159</a></sup>, it is possible that abnormal innervation of the intestine contributes to WS gastrointestinal phenotypes, such as dysmotility and chronic constipation.</p>
<p id="P34">LIM domain kinase 1 (<em>LIMK1</em>) regulates actin cytoskeleton assembly and disassembly and has been linked with visual spatial cognitive ability in individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R64">64</a></sup> and in the general population<sup><a href="#R160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R160">160</a></sup>. <em>Limk1</em><sup>–/–</sup> mice show visuospatial deficits, altered dendritic spine morphology and reduced synaptic plasticity, leading to reduced long-term memory. <em>Limk1</em> expression can be upregulated by both brain-derived neurotrophic factor<sup><a href="#R161" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R161">161</a></sup> and cAMP response element-binding protein<sup><a href="#R162" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R162">162</a></sup>, suggesting potential therapeutic avenues.</p>
<p id="P35">Syntaxin 1A (STX1A) is a key member of the protein complex that mediates exocytic vesicle fusion, thereby allowing the release of neurotransmitters into the synapse. Neuropsychiatric disorders found in individuals with WS have been associated with <em>STX1A</em> variants<sup><a href="#R163" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R163">163</a>,<a href="#R164" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R164">164</a></sup>. Insulin secretion from the pancreas is also dependent on exocytosis<sup><a href="#R165" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R165">165</a></sup>, and STX1A levels are indeed reduced in members of the general population with type 2 diabetes mellitus<sup><a href="#R166" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R166">166</a></sup>. Diabetes is common in adults with WS<sup><a href="#R167" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R167">167</a></sup>, suggesting a possible physiological link with <em>STX1A</em> hemizygosity. As the <em>MLXIPL</em> gene, which encodes the ChREBP transcription factor that regulates both glucose<sup><a href="#R168" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R168">168</a>,<a href="#R169" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R169">169</a></sup> and lipid metabolism<sup><a href="#R170" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R170">170</a></sup>, is also located within the WSCR, both ChREBP and STX1A could contribute to the metabolic phenotypes in WS.</p>
<p id="P36">Bialleleic missense variation in DNAJC30, (DNAJ heat shock protein 40 family (Hsp40)) member 30 was recently associated with Leber’s hereditary optic neuropathy in humans<sup><a href="#R171" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R171">171</a></sup>, a mitochondrial condition. Similar features have not, to date, been reported in WS. Bialleleic deletion of this gene in mice also produces mitochondrial dysfunction and behavioral changes<sup><a href="#R172" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R172">172</a></sup>. More work is needed to investigate the impact of isolated hemideletion in humans, but potential impact of this gene on neurodevelopment should be considered.</p></section></section></section><section id="S13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Diagnosis, screening and prevention</h2>
<section id="S14"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Clinical diagnosis</h3>
<p id="P37">WS is a multisystem disorder with a broad but characteristic pattern of organ involvement (<a href="#F1" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>), including a distinctive facial appearance<sup><a href="#R173" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R173">173</a>–<a href="#R175" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R175">175</a></sup> (<a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>). As there is no newborn screening for WS, clinical consideration of the diagnosis is prompted by the presence of suggestive signs and/or symptoms. Below, we outline the most common presenting features prompting consideration of a WS diagnosis. Of note, the extent and exact distribution of system involvement can vary considerably from patient to patient.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F5"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5. Facial features of children and adults with Williams syndrome of different ethnic backgrounds.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6029/9437774/71b22322f98e/nihms-1827175-f0003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1024" width="1800" alt="Figure 5."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P156">Facial photos of individuals with molecularly-confirmed Williams syndrome of different racial and/or ethnic backgrounds aged 2 months to 52 years. Distinctive features in infants and children include broad forehead, peri-orbital fullness, flat bridge of the nose, full cheeks, long philtrum, and a small delicate chin. Many adolescents and adults continue to have micrognathia, and the face often elongates over time while the nasal bridge is no longer flat, and there is fullness of the lips with a wide mouth (especially appreciated when smiling).</p>
<p id="P157">Parents or caregivers for all pictured individuals signed consent for publication of their family member’s image. The presence of more male than female photos in adolescents and adults solely reflects availability of subjects.</p>
<p id="P158">Note: This material is labeled as <a href="#F4" class="usa-link">Fig. 4</a> in the published version of the paper DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a>.</p></figcaption></figure><section id="S15"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Craniofacial differences.</h4>
<p id="P38">Individuals with WS often present with facial features that are not typical for their family. Prominent features in infants and young children include a broad forehead, peri-orbital fullness, flat bridge of the nose, full cheeks, long philtrum, and a small delicate chin. Adolescents and adults often continue to have micrognathia, but the face elongates over time, the nasal bridge is no longer flat, and there is fullness of the lips with a wide mouth (especially appreciated when smiling). Children and adults of different ethnic backgrounds from Brazil with molecularly confirmed WS are depicted in <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>.</p></section><section id="S16"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Cardiovascular anomalies.</h4>
<p id="P39">Cardiovascular disease in WS typically presents with a heart murmur. Evaluation in children most commonly reveals SVAS and/or stenosis of the main or branch pulmonary arteries. Pulmonary vascular disease is often less prominent in older individuals with WS. Other WS cardiovascular features, such as stenosis in other vessels, septal defects, hypertension, vascular stiffness or EKG abnormalities, are generally not the reason for referral but may be present at the time of diagnosis.</p></section><section id="S17"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Hypercalcaemia.</h4>
<p id="P40">Actionable hypercalcaemia (serum calcium &gt;12.0 mg/dl) is seen in 5–10% of children with WS and when present usually occurs between 6 and 30 months of age<sup><a href="#R176" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R176">176</a></sup>. While some children with hypercalcaemia are irritable and show poor oral intake, other cases are detected incidentally by laboratory testing.</p></section><section id="S18"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Growth concerns.</h4>
<p id="P41">On average, children and adults with WS are shorter than expected for age<sup><a href="#R177" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R177">177</a></sup>. Once a diagnosis is made, WS-specific growth charts are available for plotting children’s expected growth<sup><a href="#R178" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R178">178</a>,<a href="#R179" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R179">179</a></sup>. In addition, many infants with WS exhibit prolonged colic may have difficulty feeding due to oral motor delays or sensitivities, and have difficulty gaining weight.</p></section><section id="S19"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Developmental delay, intellectual disability and behavioural profile.</h4>
<p id="P42">Developmental delay is almost universal and 75% of older children and adults with WS have intellectual disability (IQ &lt;70)<sup><a href="#R178" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R178">178</a></sup>, with most remaining individuals displaying borderline IQ (70–79) and/or more specific neuropsychological impairments<sup><a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>,<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P43">Many experienced parents almost immediately notice differences from typically developing infants, but other parents may not become concerned until they realize their child with WS is not meeting motor or language milestones. Features of ASD may also lead to referral.</p></section></section><section id="S20"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Differential diagnosis</h3>
<p id="P44">It is important to distinguish WS from other syndromes with overlapping features. Certain highly suggestive features (such as SVAS, hypercalcemia and characteristic facial features) exist that, when seen in combination by an experienced examiner, readily yield a correct clinical diagnosis of WS. And though a few other disorders are evocative of WS, in that they have a similar pattern of organ involvement (such as Fetal Alcohol syndrome, Rasopathies, FG syndrome), detailed examination of their specific features reveals distinct differences from WS. Some individuals come to medical attention, however, due to a single prominent feature. Depending on the specific presenting symptom, the differential diagnosis varies and <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table 1</a> is presented to aid practitioners in that setting. A clinical suspicion of WS should always be confirmed with genetic testing (see below).</p></section><section id="S21"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Testing approaches</h3>
<p id="P45">The most widely used laboratory methods available to detect the 7q11.23 microdeletion include FISH, polymorphic microsatellite markers, multiplex ligation-dependent probe amplification (MLPA) and chromosomal microarray analysis (CMA) (<a href="#T1" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>). CMA is the only current method that does not require the clinician to suspect a specific diagnosis of WS prior to testing. In addition to providing mapping for deletion boundaries and offering the ability to detect atypical deletions, CMA can also identify additional CNV events elsewhere in the genome. MLPA and polymorphic microsatellite markers are often used in low- and middle-income developing countries owing to their lower cost than FISH and CMA<sup><a href="#R22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R22">22</a>,<a href="#R23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R23">23</a>,<a href="#R180" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R180">180</a>,<a href="#R181" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R181">181</a></sup>.</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="T1"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 1.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p id="P163">Overview of methods for diagnosing Williams syndrome</p></div>

<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Noninvasive prenatal testing</td>
