</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Facial recognition software</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Cost varies with some free software available online</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Diagnosis is limited by number of photographs available in database<br>May have different efficacy based on race or ethnicity of patient</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Whole-exome sequencing</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Deletion detection performed in research settings</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Currently used clinically for single-nucleotide variants in most cases High cost Lowest accuracy for deletion detection</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Whole-genome sequencing</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Combined single-nucleotide variant and CNV or structural variant detection performed in research settings</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">High cost, slow turnaround in some settings</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="TFN1"><p id="P164">WS, Williams syndrome; CNV, copy number variation; Mb, million base pairs</p></div></div></section><p id="P46">Newer technologies, such as facial recognition software<sup><a href="#R182" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R182">182</a>–<a href="#R184" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R184">184</a></sup>, may help focus the differential diagnosis and have been evaluated in individuals from various racial and ethnic backgrounds; the diagnostic precision of the software will likely continue to improve over time. Additional testing (gene sequencing or trinucleotide repeat expansion testing) may be indicated based on the differential diagnosis (<a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplementary table 1</a>). In the future, whole-genome sequencing may provide CNV and single-nucleotide polymorphism detection in a single test that will simultaneously assess for WS and other possible differential diagnoses.</p></section><section id="S22"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Recurrence risk</h3>
<p id="P47">If a parent has WS, the risk of WS in offspring is 50% for each pregnancy. However, owing to the complex medical and neurodevelopmental challenges associated with WS, few adults with WS have children.The recurrence risk for couples where neither parent has clinical findings of WS is extremely low<sup><a href="#R185" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R185">185</a></sup>, since the 7q11.23 microdeletion arises <em>de novo</em> in the vast majority of cases. For these couples, parental testing for a 7q11.23 microdeletion is not indicated and neither is invasive prenatal testing of subsequent pregnancies (although many parents opt for the latter, especially in countries where molecular diagnostic testing is widely available). However, there are rare reports of recurrence in phenotypically normal parents. This recurrence is likely attributed to parental mosaicism<sup><a href="#R186" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R186">186</a></sup> or to one parent carrying an inversion of the WSCR<sup><a href="#R185" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R185">185</a></sup>. The inversion has been associated with an approximately five-fold increase in risk of having a child with WS in each pregnancy; nevertheless, testing for the presence of inversion in phenotypically normal parents is not recommended, because their recurrence risk — despite being five times higher — remains well below 0.1%<sup><a href="#R42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R42">42</a>,<a href="#R44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R44">44</a>,<a href="#R185" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R185">185</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="S23"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Pregnancy and prenatal testing</h3>
<p id="P48">Limited clinical information exists on pregnancies in women with WS<sup><a href="#R187" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R187">187</a>,<a href="#R188" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R188">188</a></sup>. Review of this literature suggests that mothers and fetuses (affected by WS or not) may require close monitoring, especially with regard to the maternal cardiovascular system.</p>
<p id="P49">In addition, there is no routine prenatal test that adequately screens for WS, although prenatal ultrasonography can sometimes detect relevant fetal anomalies. The most common prenatal finding is nonspecific, namely intrauterine growth retardation<sup><a href="#R189" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R189">189</a></sup>. Various cardiovascular anomalies also can be seen and range from nonspecific (for example, ventricular septal defect) to nearly pathognomonic for elastin arteriopathy (for example, SVAS, although this finding is quite difficult to make by prenatal ultrasound)<sup><a href="#R165" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R165">165</a></sup>. The prenatal detection of growth retardation combined with any cardiovascular defect may warrant performing high-resolution prenatal ultrasonography and genetic testing.</p>
<p id="P50">Noninvasive prenatal testing (NIPT) involves sequencing of fetal DNA circulating in the maternal circulation and can detect common fetal chromosomal aneuploidies in the first trimester. Currently, as even enhanced NIPT platforms can only detect deletions &gt;3 Mb, they cannot be used to diagnose WS<sup><a href="#R189" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R189">189</a></sup>. However, further technical advances in the NIPT technology are likely to enhance prenatal diagnosis and therefore affect the epidemiology of WS in the future. In addition, whole-genome sequencing may eventually be utilized to perform combined single-nucleotide polymorphism, copy number variant and structural variant detection in fetal samples. Prenatal diagnosis offers the opportunity to provide genetic counseling to families sooner, allowing them to avoid a diagnostic odyssey that can potentially last months and years after the child’s birth.</p></section></section><section id="S24"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Management</h2>
<p id="P51">The management of various aspects of WS has been extensively outlined in numerous research studies, reviews, and guidelines<sup><a href="#R173" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R173">173</a>,<a href="#R174" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R174">174</a>,<a href="#R176" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R176">176</a>–<a href="#R178" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R178">178</a>,<a href="#R190" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R190">190</a>–<a href="#R195" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R195">195</a></sup>.Here, we focus on the management of three key areas where improved therapeutics would have the greatest potential to improve health outcomes: elastin-associated vasculopathy, hypertension, and intellectual disability, social functioning and anxiety.</p>
<section id="S25"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Elastin-associated vasculopathy</h3>
<p id="P52">As mentioned previously, individuals with elastin insufficiency can develop focal stenosis and other vascular features. Vascular disease severity varies among persons with WS, with ~20% requiring intervention for SVAS, for example, while 30–40% have little to no stenosis in this location. At present, large vessel stenosis is predominantly managed surgically. To alleviate SVAS, patch aortoplasty is the most common approach<sup><a href="#R196" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R196">196</a></sup> and has undergone several technical improvements over time, evolving from the single-patch method<sup><a href="#R197" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R197">197</a>,<a href="#R198" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R198">198</a></sup> to the pantaloon-shaped patch that enlarges the aorta and two aortic sinuses<sup><a href="#R199" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R199">199</a></sup>. The most advanced method, which involves applying a patch to each of the three aortic sinuses<sup><a href="#R200" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R200">200</a></sup>, exhibits notably lower residual pressure gradients and reoperation rates<sup><a href="#R201" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R201">201</a></sup> than the single-patch approach. Stenoses of pulmonary arteries can be treated with angioplasty, but catheter-based interventions on other arteries are often unsuccessful<sup><a href="#R11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R11">11</a>,<a href="#R90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R90">90</a>,<a href="#R202" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R202">202</a></sup>. Surveillance of stenosis occurs through regular examinations by a cardiologist and associated imaging and should continue throughout an individual’s lifetime.</p>
<p id="P53">Individuals with WS experience increased rates of cardiovascular collapse with and without anaesthesia<sup><a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">10</a>,<a href="#R12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R12">12</a></sup>, although the mechanism of this phenomenon is not completely understood. Young individuals and those with the most severe cardiovascular features (that is, biventricular outflow tract obstruction<sup><a href="#R10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R10">10</a></sup>) seem to have the highest risk, although some individuals with only minimal stenosis suffered sudden death in the setting of anaesthesia. This severe outcome may be influenced by various risk factors, including anatomical anomaly of the coronaries and reduction in perfusion pressure at induction and/or maintenance of anaesthesia. Therefore, careful preoperative screening should be performed to assess the anaesthesia-associated cardiovascular risk, and administration of intraoperative anaesthesia should ideally be provided by an anaesthesia team with knowledge of WS anaesthesia risks<sup><a href="#R93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R93">93</a>–<a href="#R95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R95">95</a>,<a href="#R203" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R203">203</a>,<a href="#R204" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R204">204</a></sup>. Care should be taken not to acutely lower blood pressure at induction of anaesthesia in order to maintain adequate perfusion of the coronaries during this sensitive time.</p>
<p id="P54">Studies suggest that novel pharmacological therapies may improve elastin-associated vasculopathy. Minoxidil, an ATP-dependent potassium channel opener, has received considerable attention based on evidence that it increased elastin production in cellular studies<sup><a href="#R205" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R205">205</a></sup> and in animal models<sup><a href="#R206" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R206">206</a>,<a href="#R207" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R207">207</a></sup> of genetic elastin deficiency and that it ameliorated age-related degeneration of elastic fibres in mice<sup><a href="#R208" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R208">208</a>,<a href="#R209" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R209">209</a></sup>. However, a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled trial (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/ct2/show/NCT00876200" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">NCT00876200</a>) investigating the effect of a year-long minoxidil treatment in 17 individuals with WS failed to show improvement in the primary outcome measure (carotid intima-media thickness)<sup><a href="#R210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R210">210</a></sup>. This trial did show an increase in lumen size (a secondary finding, in line with previous mouse studies<sup><a href="#R207" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R207">207</a></sup>) over the same time interval, along with an expected common adverse effect of hypertrichosis<sup><a href="#R210" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R210">210</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P55">Alternative therapeutic approaches may target various regulatory pathways that influence elastin expression. For example, both the coding region and the 3ʹ UTR of the <em>ELN</em> mRNA are enriched in binding sites for miR-29 and miR-15, two microRNAs that are upregulated in the late postnatal aorta, at the same time that the level of mature <em>ELN</em> mRNA decreases<sup><a href="#R82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R82">82</a></sup>. Antagonism of miR-29 increases elastin expression in haploinsufficient cells and bioengineered vessels<sup><a href="#R211" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R211">211</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="S26"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Hypertension</h3>
<p id="P56">Elastin insufficiency is also associated with hypertension, but the risk of clinically significant blood pressure increases is modified by deletion size and <em>NCF1</em> gene dosage<sup><a href="#R91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R91">91</a>,<a href="#R116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R116">116</a></sup>. Blood pressure should be measured in both arms and at least one leg due to possible right arm flow acceleration (the so-called Coanda effect) and/or coarctation of the aorta<sup><a href="#R212" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R212">212</a></sup>. If consistent blood pressure elevations or pressure differentials (in the arms or as an arm-leg discrepancy, respectively) are detected, additional imaging of the heart and ascending aorta (by echocardiography) and the renal/abdominal vasculature (by Doppler ultrasonography, CT angiography or MRI/MR angiography) should be considered (when available), to assess the presence of focal or long-segment stenosis that affects renal perfusion and may benefit from surgical intervention<sup><a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>,<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">9</a></sup>. Auscultation of the abdomen can also reveal a bruit (abnormal sound) in the region of the stenosis.</p>
<p id="P57">Blood pressure elevation often starts in childhood and increases in frequency with age<sup><a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">8</a>,<a href="#R9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R9">9</a>,<a href="#R116" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R116">116</a>,<a href="#R213" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R213">213</a></sup>. Unlike stenosis, most cases of hypertension are treated pharmacologically<sup><a href="#R5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R5">5</a>,<a href="#R8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R8">8</a></sup>. Currently, there is no expert consensus on the best antihypertensive medication for individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R214" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R214">214</a></sup>. Similarly, work in <em>Eln</em><sup>+/–</sup> mice revealed no superior drug class for treating elastin-mediated hypertension<sup><a href="#R215" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R215">215</a></sup>. However, because blood pressure influences lumen size, which in turn affects end-organ blood flow, care must be taken not to decrease pressure to the extent that lumen size, and therefore end-organ blood flow, is pathologically diminished.</p>
<p id="P58">For this reason, among others, diuretics may not be the ideal or initial choice for blood pressure management in individuals with WS. In addition, when reno-vascular causes of hypertension are suspected, angiotensin receptor blockers and angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitors should be used with caution. As hypertension risk has been linked to ROS production through the NOX signaling pathway, potential therapeutic strategies could include treatments that limit ROS production or affect signaling through ROS<sup><a href="#R216" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R216">216</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="S27"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Intellectual and social functioning</h3>
<p id="P59">Deletion of multiple genes within the WSCR likely contribute to intellectual disability, altered social functioning and anxiety, although the roles of <em>GTF2I</em> and <em>GTF2IRD1</em> (as outlined above) are the best described to date.</p>
<p id="P60">WS is associated with developmental delay (the expected developmental milestones are shown in <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>). The delays typically lead to mild to moderate intellectual disability, although a few individuals have severe intellectual disability or, at the other extreme, average intellectual ability<sup><a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>,<a href="#R217" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R217">217</a></sup>. This overall level of ability masks a phenotypic pattern of strengths and weaknesses relative to expectations for overall level of intellectual ability. In general, language and nonverbal reasoning abilities are stronger than expected for overall intellectual ability, whereas visuospatial construction (for example, handwriting and block construction) is considerably weaker than expected<sup><a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>,<a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>,<a href="#R218" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R218">218</a></sup>. Visuospatial construction is facilitated by visual processing regions. Interestingly, research using resting-state functional MRI showed that for children in the general population, the intraparietal sulcus is functionally connected to more superior-anterior visual processing regions, whereas it is instead connected to social regions in children with WS<sup><a href="#R160" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R160">160</a></sup>. The lack of this specific functional connection in individuals with WS contributes to their considerable weakness in visual spatial construction<sup><a href="#R219" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R219">219</a></sup>. A more detailed description of the pattern of relative strengths and weaknesses within the language domain is provided in <a href="#T2" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6. Developmental milestones for very young children with Williams syndrome.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/6029/9437774/88e21a1c6dfb/nihms-1827175-f0004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1169" width="1800" alt="Figure 6."></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P159">Median ages (in months) and 90<sup>th</sup> and 10<sup>th</sup> percentiles for attainment of various gross motor, fine motor, cognitive and language milestones by very young children with Williams syndrome (yellow bars) relative to typically developing children (aqua bars)<sup><a href="#R154" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R154">154</a>,<a href="#R294" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R294">294</a>–<a href="#R302" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R302">302</a></sup>. Numbers in red typeface indicate median age; numbers in black typeface indicate 90<sup>th</sup> percentile (to the left of the median) and 10<sup>th</sup> percentile (to the right of the median). TD, typically developing.</p>
<p id="P160">Note: <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> was redrawn by the Nature Reviews Disease Primers art editor and permission for reproduction of the final figure was not granted by the publisher. This material is labeled as <a href="#F5" class="usa-link">Fig. 5</a> in the published version of the paper DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a></p></figcaption></figure><section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="T2"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 2.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p id="P165">Pattern of relative strengths and weaknesses in individuals with WS<sup><a href="#TFN2" class="usa-link">a</a></sup>.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="box" rules="all">
<colgroup span="1">
<col align="left" valign="middle" span="1">
<col align="left" valign="middle" span="1">
<col align="left" valign="middle" span="1">
<col align="left" valign="middle" span="1">
</colgroup>
<thead><tr>
<th align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Category</th>
<th align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ability</th>
<th align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Details</th>
<th align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Level relative to overall intellectual ability</th>
</tr></thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td rowspan="3" align="left" valign="top" colspan="1">General pattern</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Verbal</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Vocabulary breadth and verbal analogies</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Stronger than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Nonverbal reasoning</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Matrix reasoning and pattern completion</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Stronger than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Visual-spatial construction</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Drawing, writing and block construction</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Considerably weaker than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td rowspan="7" align="left" valign="top" colspan="1">Language profile</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Phonological processing</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Knowledge of the sound structure of language and ability to manipulate that structure</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Stronger than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Vocabulary breadth</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Concrete vocabulary, picture identification and naming</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Stronger than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Verbal short-term memory</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ability to repeat back what was said verbatim</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Stronger than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Grammatical ability</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ability to speak grammatically and understand sentences someone else produced</td>

</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Verbal working memory</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ability to repeat back what was said in a different order (for example, in the reverse order) from the original</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">At the expected level</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Vocabulary depth</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Ability to define words accurately and specifically</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">At the expected level</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Relational vocabulary</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Understanding and use of spatial, temporal, and quantitative concepts and of complex conjunctions (for example, however) or disjunctions (for example, neither nor)</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Considerably weaker than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td rowspan="3" align="left" valign="top" colspan="1"></td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Nonliteral language</td>

</tr>
<tr>

<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Considerably weaker than expected</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Comprehension monitoring</td>
<td align="left" valign="top" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Evaluating whether one understood what was said or read and then taking appropriate action if one had not understood</td>

</tr>
</tbody>
</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/T2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="TFN2">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline" id="P166">Strengths and weaknesses relative to overall intellectual ability for broad categories of intellectual ability and within the language domain.</p>
</div></div></section><p id="P61">Current information indicates that cognitive ability remains stable, at least to mid-adulthood<sup><a href="#R220" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R220">220</a>–<a href="#R223" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R223">223</a></sup>. There is a possibility of IQ decline in older adults<sup><a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">34</a></sup>, but the data sets are limited.</p>
<p id="P62">WS is also associated with a characteristic behavioural profile that is consistent across both Western and Eastern cultures<sup><a href="#R224" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R224">224</a></sup>. At its most basic level, this profile includes gregariousness and hypersociability (or ‘overfriendliness’), accompanied by attention problems (often meeting diagnostic criteria for Attention Deficit Hyperactivity Disorder or ADHD), social problems, anxiety and emotional overreactivity<sup><a href="#R15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R15">15</a>,<a href="#R16" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R16">16</a>,<a href="#R225" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R225">225</a>–<a href="#R229" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R229">229</a></sup>. Mastery motivation (that is, willingness to persist on a task one finds moderately difficult) is typically very limited<sup><a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>,<a href="#R227" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R227">227</a></sup>. The most common psychiatric problems associated with WS, and estimates of their prevalence based on meeting formal diagnostic criteria outlined in the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders IV (DSM), are delineated in <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="F7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7. Prevalence of psychiatric diagnoses in individuals with Williams syndrome throughout the life span.</h4>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/F7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p id="P161">The genetic deletion in Williams syndrome (WS) has cascading effects on social, educational and vocational opportunities and on psychopathology. Estimated prevalence of psychiatric disorders in preschool-aged children, school-aged children and adolescents, and adults with Williams syndrome<sup><a href="#R154" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R154">154</a>,<a href="#R213" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R213">213</a>,<a href="#R266" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R266">266</a>,<a href="#R303" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R303">303</a>,<a href="#R304" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R304">304</a></sup> is depicted. Studies with participant over a wide age range that did not report results separately for children and adults were excluded.<sup><a href="#R236" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R236">236</a>,<a href="#R305" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R305">305</a>–<a href="#R307" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R307">307</a></sup> The term ‘anxiety disorders’ includes generalized anxiety disorder, specific phobia, separation anxiety or social anxiety, and panic disorder with agoraphobia.<sup>a</sup>Most individuals with WS who have autism spectrum disorder (ASD) fit in Wing and Gould’s<sup><a href="#R292" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R292">292</a></sup> active-but-odd subtype of ASD<sup><a href="#R231" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R231">231</a></sup> rather than the aloof subtype.</p>
<p id="P162">Note: <a href="#F7" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a> was drawn by the Nature Reviews Disease Primers art editor and permission for reproduction of the final figure was not granted by the publisher. This material is labeled <a href="#F6" class="usa-link">Fig. 6</a> in the published version of the paper for the final figure at DOI: <a href="https://doi.org/10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">10.1038/s41572-021-00276-z</a></p></figcaption></figure><p id="P63">In most cases, the previously described learning and developmental differences are addressed by the school system through specialized education and therapy services, which are outlined below. In general, physical, occupational, and speech and language therapy are recommended for infants through school-aged children in the USA<sup><a href="#R178" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R178">178</a>,<a href="#R230" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R230">230</a></sup> and in Australia<sup><a href="#R192" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R192">192</a></sup>. By contrast, according to a parent survey in the UK, only 20–40% of school children with WS receive these services<sup><a href="#R231" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R231">231</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P64">The behavioural and psychiatric components of WS are managed with the help of structural and environmental supports at home and in school and vocational settings, including counselling and behavioural therapies<sup><a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>,<a href="#R156" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R156">156</a>,<a href="#R178" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R178">178</a>,<a href="#R232" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R232">232</a></sup>. For individuals whose symptoms greatly affect their daily functioning and quality of life (QOL), referral to a psychiatrist for consideration of pharmacological intervention may be indicated<sup><a href="#R178" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R178">178</a></sup>. The anxiety associated with WS has been particularly difficult to treat. Trials are needed to identify appropriate therapeutics for anxiety, although, as has been shown for typically developing individuals, cognitive behaviour therapy seems promising as an effective treatment for anxiety in WS<sup><a href="#R233" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R233">233</a>,<a href="#R234" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R234">234</a></sup>. The same medication classes available for anxiolysis in the general population have been studied in small series of individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R235" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R235">235</a>,<a href="#R236" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R236">236</a></sup> and are already widely used in clinical practice, but result in varying symptom relief. Furthermore, the risk:benefit ratio may be more unfavorable for some of these medications in those with WS, as reviewed by Thom et al<sup><a href="#R237" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R237">237</a></sup>. Specifically, anxiety, mood, cardiac function and blood pressure all need to be monitored given the overlap between common side effects of ADHD medication in the general population and the psychological and physical difficulties associated with WS.</p></section><section id="S28"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Educational and vocational provision</h3>
<p id="P65">In high-income countries, a large proportion of primary school-aged children with WS are educated in mainstream schools and spend at least part of the day in classes with typically developing agemates; these proportions decrease considerably for secondary school<sup><a href="#R231" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R231">231</a>,<a href="#R238" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R238">238</a></sup>. Although there are no data specifically for children with WS, in low-income and middle-income countries, school attendance is much less common for children with disabilities than for typically developing children<sup><a href="#R239" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R239">239</a></sup>, due not only to financial difficulties but also to discriminatory and negative views of persons with intellectual disability<sup><a href="#R240" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R240">240</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P66">Postsecondary education for individuals with WS is very limited worldwide, although opportunities are increasing in some high-income countries. In a US survey whose respondents were on average upper-middle class, 29% of individuals with WS had attended a postsecondary education programme<sup><a href="#R241" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R241">241</a></sup>. These programmes typically focus on combinations of academics, vocational training (including job placement and coaching) and independent living skills.</p>
<p id="P67">After completing school, most adults with WS continue to live with their parent or another relative; &lt;10% live independently<sup><a href="#R241" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R241">241</a>,<a href="#R242" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R242">242</a></sup>. Only 38–54% of adults are working at least part-time, generally in a special employment arrangement, either for pay or as a volunteer<sup><a href="#R241" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R241">241</a>,<a href="#R242" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R242">242</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P68">Within the academic sphere, reading skills are considerably stronger than mathematics skills<sup><a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>,<a href="#R243" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R243">243</a></sup> and range from an inability to read at all to reading comprehension at age or grade level<sup><a href="#R243" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R243">243</a>–<a href="#R246" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R246">246</a></sup>. About 30% of adolescents and adults with Williams syndrome have functional reading capability<sup><a href="#R244" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R244">244</a></sup>. Children who were taught to read using systematic phonics instruction, which emphasizes letter–sound relations, read and comprehend significantly better than those taught using other instructional approaches<sup><a href="#R246" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R246">246</a>,<a href="#R247" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R247">247</a></sup>. There has been no research on written composition, and very little is known about the mathematical abilities of individuals with WS<sup><a href="#R248" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R248">248</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P69">Within the adaptive behaviour domain, socialization and communication skills are stronger than daily living and motor skills for children and adolescents with WS<sup><a href="#R249" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R249">249</a></sup>. In longitudinal studies, adaptive behaviour standard scores declined significantly during childhood<sup><a href="#R250" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R250">250</a></sup> and in adulthood<sup><a href="#R222" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R222">222</a></sup>, owing to stagnation or failure to increase adaptive skills at the rate needed to maintain a consistent standard score over time. For adolescents and adults with WS, adaptive behaviour is more limited than expected for their IQ<sup><a href="#R251" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R251">251</a>,<a href="#R252" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R252">252</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P70">High but realistic parental expectations combined with better behavioural regulation<sup><a href="#R234" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R234">234</a></sup> and higher levels of mastery motivation are expected to positively affect both adaptive behaviour and academic achievement<sup><a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a></sup>. Even so, in a survey of teachers, most indicated they were not given adequate resources to teach children with WS<sup><a href="#R238" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R238">238</a></sup>, and most parents surveyed thought their child’s teachers had little knowledge about WS<sup><a href="#R238" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R238">238</a></sup>. Much more research is needed on effective instructional strategies for individuals with WS at all educational levels<sup><a href="#R253" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R253">253</a></sup>.</p></section></section><section id="S29"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Quality of life</h2>
<p id="P71">The combination of intellectual disability, medical problems, and behavioural, psychological and adaptive impairments leads to considerable limitations on QOL in individuals with WS. Parents and teachers of children with WS report difficulties with peers, including problems establishing and maintaining friendships and increased social exclusion or isolation<sup><a href="#R254" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R254">254</a></sup>. Although social vulnerability is high, self-awareness is limited<sup><a href="#R255" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R255">255</a></sup>. Seventy-three percent of parents reported victimization of their child<sup><a href="#R256" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R256">256</a></sup>, with the social interaction style of individuals with WS contributing significantly to their social vulnerability<sup><a href="#R257" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R257">257</a></sup>. Even adolescents and adults with WS are typically not cognizant of stranger danger<sup><a href="#R258" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R258">258</a>,<a href="#R259" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R259">259</a></sup>. Intervention studies addressing these issues<sup><a href="#R258" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R258">258</a>,<a href="#R260" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R260">260</a></sup> are both rare and crucial<sup><a href="#R229" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R229">229</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P72">Nearly all children with WS require multiple encounters with the healthcare system for management of medical or surgical problems<sup><a href="#R261" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R261">261</a></sup>. These encounters place a financial and emotional burden on the individuals with WS and their families<sup><a href="#R262" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R262">262</a></sup> and often contribute to anticipatory anxiety about medical encounters and procedures<sup><a href="#R263" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R263">263</a></sup>. Several studies of adults with WS ≥30 years of age demonstrated an increased frequency, variety and severity of medical morbidities<sup><a href="#R33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R33">33</a>,<a href="#R34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R34">34</a>,<a href="#R213" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R213">213</a></sup>, and this trajectory accelerates among those &gt;65 years of age (personal observation).</p>
<p id="P73">Prominent adult issues include cardiovascular disease, obesity (with/without lipoedema), diabetes mellitus, incontinence, hearing loss, consequences of poor oral health, gastrointestinal problems (including diverticulitis), decreased bone mineral density and sleep apnea<sup><a href="#R174" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R174">174</a>,<a href="#R213" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R213">213</a></sup>. Psychiatric concerns are often paramount. Health issues such as these may result in narrowed residential and vocational placements, restricted mobility and physical activity and, collectively, they promote further social isolation. Medical complications require focused management based on established guidelines, whereas general health could be improved by participation in programmes that promote healthy eating and increasing physical exercise activity to recommended adult levels<sup><a href="#R264" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R264">264</a>,<a href="#R265" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R265">265</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="P74">The presence of a child with WS may affect the QOL of other family members. Both generalized anxiety disorder<sup><a href="#R266" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R266">266</a></sup> and borderline or clinically significant levels of stress<sup><a href="#R267" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R267">267</a>,<a href="#R268" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R268">268</a></sup> occur in a significantly higher proportion of mothers of children with WS than expected for same-aged women in the general population. Sensory modulation problems are very common among children with WS and are associated with more difficult temperament, more limited adaptive behaviour and emotion regulation difficulties and behavioural problems<sup><a href="#R269" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R269">269</a></sup>. In turn, child behavioural problems (especially externalizing problems) contribute to increased maternal stress<sup><a href="#R267" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R267">267</a>,<a href="#R270" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R270">270</a></sup> or challenges with raising the child (owing to, for example, the child’s difficulties with social skills or obsessions)<sup><a href="#R271" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R271">271</a></sup>. The discrepancy between the general perception that children with WS are happy and have ‘easy’ temperaments and the reality that most children with WS have relatively difficult temperaments is in itself likely to increase maternal stress<sup><a href="#R227" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R227">227</a></sup>. Worries regarding the child’s future are very common among family members and mothers in particular<sup><a href="#R231" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R231">231</a>,<a href="#R271" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R271">271</a>,<a href="#R272" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R272">272</a></sup>. At the same time, most mothers also reported positive aspects of having a child with WS, including that the child brought joy and changed the mother’s outlook on life<sup><a href="#R271" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R271">271</a></sup> (<a href="#BX1" class="usa-link">Box 1</a>).</p>
<section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="BX1"><h3 class="obj_head">Box 1. Patient perspective (family interview).</h3>
<p id="P167">Molly (not her real name) is a 30-year-old woman with Williams syndrome who lives with her parents in a rural town in Australia. She has mild to moderate intellectual disability and the typical Williams syndrome personality. She completed school in a mainstream setting for the first six years, then moved to a special school. She currently works 3 days a week in sheltered employment after failed attempts to work in mainstream employment due to bullying. Molly enjoys music, softball and line dancing. Molly has struggled with many of the medical and psychological difficulties associated with Williams syndrome, but due to her determined nature and strong family support, she has made many wonderful life achievements. Please see <a href="#SD1" class="usa-link">Supplementary box 3</a> for the full interview.</p>
<p id="P168">Interviewer: Do you feel that others treat you any differently because you have Williams syndrome?</p>
<p id="P169">Molly: The way people talk to you is different, and they talk to mum instead of me… about my medical things …and I am like: “Hello, I am over here. Mum doesn’t have Williams syndrome. I do”.</p>
<p id="P170">Interviewer: What are the good things about having Williams syndrome?</p>
<p id="P171">Molly: I make people laugh and feel good. At home I am demanding. I love older people and their stories. I love to hear what they were up to back in the day.</p>
<p id="P172">Interviewer: What are the not so good/hard things about having Williams syndrome?</p>
<p id="P173">Molly: Lots of people don’t know about Williams syndrome. Most doctors don’t know about Williams syndrome. When I go to hospital, I always take an information sheet [about Williams syndrome].</p>
<p id="P174">I get bullied a lot. I think because of the way… oh this makes me feel sad… (now <em>teary) …</em>I think because of the way I act some people don’t realise it is hard to always be happy.</p>
<p id="P175">I know this might sound stupid, but I can’t tie my shoelaces. I wear shoes with elasticated shoelaces.</p>
<p id="P176">Interviewer: What is the biggest thing that having a child with Williams syndrome has taught you?</p>
<p id="P177">Parent: The biggest thing we have learnt is that a person with Williams syndrome is no different to you and me. We all have different personalities, have different needs and process information differently. We have a greater level of acceptance and empathy by having a person with Williams syndrome in our lives.</p>
<p id="P178">Interviewer: What have been the major challenges associated with having a daughter with Williams syndrome?</p>
<p id="P179">Parent: At first it is hectic. Appointments are on-going and seem to be never ending. You are always planning for the next stage. Learning how to feed and care for your child with Williams syndrome is the first step. Then moving on to therapy and the first stage of learning with early intervention and transition to school.</p>
<p id="P180">Also, stranger danger is a big concern as Molly accepts everyone as her best friend.</p>
<p id="P181">Interviewer: What is your take home message to new parents of a child with Williams syndrome?</p>
<p id="P182">Parent: Don’t be afraid to ask for support especially in the early years as there are times that you will be overwhelmed but as time goes on the positives can outweigh the negatives.</p>
<p id="P183">Interviewer: What are the three main points you want to make about Williams syndrome?</p>
<p id="P184">Parent: Williams syndrome is challenging but can be rewarding. The person with Williams syndrome is just like you and I but processes information slower. We just need to be more accepting, tolerant, non-judgmental. Treat a person with Williams syndrome with respect.</p></section></section><section id="S30"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Outlook</h2>
<p id="P75">Much has been learned about WS since its initial description, but important questions remain (<a href="#BX2" class="usa-link">Box 2</a>). These questions centre on three major themes: molecular mechanisms of disease, interindividual variability and effective treatment strategies. A more complete understanding of the genes and pathways contributing to the phenotypes of individuals with WS would allow clinicians to move beyond symptom management and to instead use precisely targeted therapies to improve organ function and ultimately health outcomes.</p>
<section class="bt xbox font-sm" id="BX2"><h3 class="obj_head">Box 2. Key questions for future work in WS.</h3>
<section id="S36"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Long-term health in Williams syndrome (WS)</h4>
<p id="P185">How do health needs change for individuals with WS across the lifespan? What recommendations can be made to optimize health of older individuals with WS?</p>
<p id="P186">Do different pathologies arise in WS at different developmental stages, and are any reversible? If treatments are designed targeting early processes, will this “normalize” future outcomes, or will it be necessary to target multiple stages and processes?</p></section><section id="S37"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Phenotypic variability</h4>
<p id="P187">What are the biological and environmental contributors to variability in outcomes in WS?</p>
<p id="P188">How do the ~20 WSCR genes without a specific phenotype designation affect health and development in people with WS? How should the combinatorial effects of multiple genes on disease variability be assessed?</p>
<p id="P189">How does variability in WSCR genes contribute to phenotypic variability in the general population?</p></section><section id="S38"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Disease mechanism</h4>
<p id="P190">What are the developmental neuropathological mechanisms underlying social and anxiety-related behaviour impairments in WS?</p>
<p id="P191">What factors impact transcription, translation, and deposition of elastin? Can those genes or pathways be harnessed to appropriately re-initiate elastin deposition outside of its normal developmental window?</p>
<p id="P192">What underlies metabolic differences in WS, such as glucose dysregulation, aberrant growth and aberrant body composition? How do changes in the genes contributing to these phenotypes affect health for people with and without WS?</p></section><section id="S39"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Interventions and treatment</h4>
<p id="P193">What interventions (medical, psychological or behavioural) would best improve quality of life for people with WS, especially pertaining to anxiety, mastery motivation and social vulnerability?</p>
<p id="P194">Should hypertension be aggressively treated in WS? What is the effect on short-term end organ perfusion and longer-term organ function?</p>
<p id="P195">What risk factors for adverse anaesthesia events need further refinement, such that management guidelines can be generated and widely shared?</p></section><section id="S40"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Research priorities for WS</h4>
<p id="P196">How can resources be developed to facilitate collaborative data collection and treatment trial coordination to optimize delivery of new treatments for people with WS?</p></section></section><section id="S31"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Molecular mechanisms of disease</h3>
<p id="P76">To date, only <em>ELN</em>, <em>GTF2I</em> and <em>GTF2IRD1</em> have been definitively linked to key phenotypes that are identifiable in individuals with WS, but even for these genes, a significant knowledge gap between disease phenotype and gene function remains. For example, published literature has not yet clarified whether the global vascular narrowing seen in WS is driven by changes in cell proliferation<sup><a href="#R99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R99">99</a></sup>, radial growth of the vessel<sup><a href="#R102" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R102">102</a></sup> or alteration in cell–matrix interactions. In addition, the characteristic hourglass-type stenosis feature in WS seems to be driven by entirely different mechanisms than the more general narrow and stiff vessel pathology seen throughout the rest of the vasculature<sup><a href="#R7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R7">7</a>,<a href="#R97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R97">97</a></sup>. While for <em>GTF2I</em> and <em>GTF2IRD1</em>, clear effects on brain development and function are evident, the direct effect of reduced gene products on neural circuits, white matter properties<sup><a href="#R273" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R273">273</a></sup>, developmental timing and brain activity is unknown.</p>
<p id="P77">To answer these questions, further studies on the roles of individual genes in human pathophysiology, at the cellular and molecular level, and in model systems are needed. In humans, a traditional approach has been to clinically characterize individuals with smaller WSCR deletions, in an effort to create more specificity for the traits associated with each gene (<a href="#F3" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). Currently, clinical exome and whole-genome sequencing are being used to identify rare single gene variation in individuals with phenotypes that overlap with WS (a phenotype to gene approach). In the future, the move toward big-data and genotype-first methods (a gene to phenotype approach) may offer new hope in the identification and further refinement of genotype–phenotype relationships that are part of the complex multisystem disorder of WS.</p>
<p id="P78">Further clarification of gene function can be obtained from studies in model systems. Cell systems offer ease of manipulation and provide precise ways to study protein–protein interactions and gene expression. However, they lack much of the complexity (for example, multiple cell types, tissue movement and endocrine signaling) needed to truly model human disease. Similarly, animal models (usually mice) may imperfectly match human disease outcomes; for example, the <em>Eln</em><sup>+/–</sup> mouse does not have SVAS and, in the case of cognitive conditions, mouse behaviours may incompletely or inaccurately correspond to human behaviours. The advent of iPSCs, tissue engineering and ‘organs in a dish’ may offer the potential to bridge some of the gaps that are present in traditional cell culture systems<sup><a href="#R68" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R68">68</a>,<a href="#R69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R69">69</a>,<a href="#R274" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R274">274</a>–<a href="#R276" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R276">276</a></sup>. As in all disease modeling, the biggest challenge lies in the ability of the <em>in vitro</em> system to truly mimic the complex <em>in vivo</em> environment. Brain organoids have shown promise for modeling developmental disorders such as autism and epilepsy<sup><a href="#R277" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R277">277</a>,<a href="#R278" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R278">278</a></sup>. Vascular organoids<sup><a href="#R279" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R279">279</a></sup> and numerous tissue engineering approaches <sup><a href="#R280" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R280">280</a></sup> are being applied to the study of blood vessels.Progress is being made but it has been difficult to design complex vascular tissues that deposit mechanically competent and mature elastin<sup><a href="#R281" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R281">281</a>,<a href="#R282" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R282">282</a></sup>. Rapid improvement in these systems is expected in the coming years as more precise genetic approaches to adapting both cellular and animal systems facilitate the study of specific outcomes.</p></section><section id="S32"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Interindividual variability</h3>
<p id="P79">Phenotypic variation is readily apparent in WS, despite the vast majority of individuals carrying the typical 1.55–1.83 Mb WS deletion. Currently, the mechanisms underlying this variability are mostly unknown. It is likely that both environmental factors and genetic modifiers contribute to the overall penetrance of specific signs and symptoms in an individual with WS.</p>
<p id="P80">The study of polygenic risk is still in its relative infancy but offers essential insight into features of WS that overlap with common health conditions in the general population<sup><a href="#R283" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R283">283</a>,<a href="#R284" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R284">284</a></sup>. Variation in genes that globally influence hypertension or IQ, for example, likely act together with genes in the WSCR in additive and synergistic ways to produce much of the observed variation<sup><a href="#R285" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R285">285</a></sup>. In this way, the study of genetic modifiers in individuals with microdeletion disorders may offer a shortcut to identify relevant disease pathways by acting as a sensitivity screen of sorts, with the notable downside being the difficulty of acquiring truly robust sample sizes.</p>
<p id="P81">Sets of polygenic risk genes for more unique WS features (such as SVAS or odynacusis<sup><a href="#R286" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R286">286</a></sup>) are currently unavailable, but if identified, they may provide insight into targetable pathways that could impact these important outcomes. For example, ~20% of all individuals with WS require surgical correction for SVAS, but if it has been established that those who do need surgery have a variation in a particular pathway, treatment could be targeted to the modifier pathway rather that to elastin insufficiency itself. Some work has already been initiated in this area<sup><a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a></sup> but larger studies covering a broader racial and ethnic distribution are needed.</p>
<p id="P82">Other, understudied areas of potential variation include epigenetic effects and somatic variation. In addition, little is known about how differences in the flanking LCR regions, which are difficult to study by current short-read sequencing methods, may affect disease outcomes. Another area requiring further study is environmental influences, prenatally and throughout life, on disease outcomes. The gut microbiome, which is potentially affected by feeding difficulties, early hospitalizations and increased medication use, also deserves consideration. Environment and gene x environment interactions, in particular, are likely to play a crucial role in educational achievement<sup><a href="#R14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R14">14</a>,<a href="#R244" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R244">244</a>,<a href="#R246" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R246">246</a>,<a href="#R247" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R247">247</a></sup> and adaptive functional outcomes <sup><a href="#R13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R13">13</a>,<a href="#R223" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R223">223</a>,<a href="#R249" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R249">249</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="S33"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Effective treatment strategies</h3>
<p id="P83">Treatment strategies can be designed using a variety of approaches: targeting the genes and/or gene products themselves, targeting functional pathways and aiming to treat disease symptoms and signs. For the gene-based strategies, CRISPR- and viral vector-based gene therapy technologies<sup><a href="#R287" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R287">287</a>,<a href="#R288" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R288">288</a></sup> are currently being studied in other rare conditions<sup><a href="#R289" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R289">289</a>,<a href="#R290" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R290">290</a></sup>. However, unlike single-gene diseases, microdeletion disorders such as WS offer particular challenges that relate to the large size of the material to be delivered and the sheer number of locations where the genes would need to be targeted to alter relevant phenotypes. It is possible that a subset of genes could be delivered using the methods previously described, which may improve efficiency of delivery and function. However, it is known that the 7q11.23 duplication syndrome<sup><a href="#R149" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R149">149</a>,<a href="#R291" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R291">291</a></sup>results from increased dosage of at least some of the genes in the WSCR, and therefore any therapeutic approach must involve carefully regulated gene expression or protein replacement. Although the ability to identify and modify target genes, either <em>in utero</em> or in early postnatal stages, offers the potential to greatly improve precise treatment in WS, much work is required before this strategy becomes a reality.</p>
<p id="P84">In the coming years, therapies are expected based on individual genes or pathways that are known to be important for influencing phenotype in WS. Studies in mouse models, for example, suggest that anti-miRNAs (such as anti-miR29a) may be useful for increasing elastin deposition<sup><a href="#R211" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R211">211</a></sup> and that influencing smooth muscle cell behaviour using inhibitors of mTOR<sup><a href="#R106" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R106">106</a></sup> or integrin β3<sup><a href="#R101" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R101">101</a></sup> could improve the vascular features of WS. Moreover, new pharmacological strategies targeting myelination have been proposed for improving neural outcomes<sup><a href="#R143" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R143">143</a></sup>. However, each of these potential strategies comes with challenges in delivery, specificity or longevity. For example, miR29a regulates multiple transcripts, so although anti-miR29a administration may lead to increased elastin message stability (and therefore increased translation)<sup><a href="#R211" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R211">211</a></sup>, other transcripts bound by miR29a may also be affected, potentially leading to other complications. Development of additional (non-rodent) WS models may be useful preclinical modalities prior to initiation of human trials. As people with WS retain one copy of each gene from the WSCR, future studies could be aimed at developing methods to target and upregulate expression of the remaining allele.</p>
<p id="P85">Additionally, patient-derived iPSCs may soon offer platforms through which to test novel therapeutics for impact on unique WS transcriptional pathways and phenotypes. Initial investigations incorporating this technology have been used to screen for medications that impact the increased smooth muscle cell proliferation seen in WS<sup><a href="#R105" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R105">105</a></sup>. Rather than a goal of preventing stenosis, future studies that focus on resolving or reducing existing stenoses may be more relevant to treatment and lead to improved clinical outcomes, especially in young children who often come to medical attention with stenosis already present. When iPS cells or gene-edited versions thereof are being considered as deliverable therapeutics themselves, the timing and delivery mechanisms will be critically important.</p>
<p id="P86">In the interim, clinicians manage specific symptoms in individuals with WS (such as hypertension, anxiety, and hypercalcaemia) using medications and interventions that have been developed for these indications in the general population, without knowing if the mechanism of disease in WS is the same. For example, several classes of antihypertensive drugs are available, and monotherapy or polytherapy can reduce blood pressure in most individuals with WS. However, the targeting of these drugs is not mechanism-based, and formal studies on which medications in these classes are best for symptom control in the WS population are lacking<sup><a href="#R214" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R214">214</a></sup>. The mechanistic and modifier studies previously defined could help narrow the options needed to pursue hypothesis-driven clinical trials. For the near term, longitudinal studies, as well as open label and double-blind randomized clinical trials, on common and high impact medical and behavioral problems are needed, the findings from which could provide valuable clinical algorithms to guide treatment.</p></section><section id="S34"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Moving forward</h3>
<p id="P87">To improve health outcomes for people with WS, a multifaceted approach is needed that brings together more investigators with expertise in the range of conditions and genes represented in WS. Newer topics, such as metabolic and immune differences<sup><a href="#R29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R29">29</a>,<a href="#R66" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R66">66</a>,<a href="#R190" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="R190">190</a></sup> in WS, are also in need of innovators. Funding is required to allow for the creation of a large international consortium that will collect prospective standardized data focused on the most relevant questions in WS and will incentivize the broad sharing of data acquired in diverse populations. Such a consortium will allow more rapid collection and dissemination of natural history data, which are needed to understand long-term outcomes and to choose appropriate endpoints for subsequent clinical trials. In addition, biospecimen collection can be optimized by the same mechanism. Ultimately, clinical trials focusing on therapies are critically needed to increase QOL for individuals with WS and their families.</p></section></section><section id="SM1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary Material</h2>

            data-ga-label="collections_radio_existing" />
            <label class="usa-radio__label" for="collections-action-dialog-existing">Add to an existing collection</label>
        </div>
    </fieldset>

    <fieldset class="usa-fieldset margin-bottom-2">
        <div class="action-panel-control-wrap new-collections-controls">
           <label for="collections-action-dialog-add-to-new" class="usa-label margin-top-0">
                Name your collection
               <abbr title="required" class="usa-hint usa-hint--required text-no-underline">*</abbr>
          </label>
          <input
            type="text"
            name="add-to-new-collection"
            id="collections-action-dialog-add-to-new"
            class="usa-input collections-action-add-to-new"
            pattern="[^&quot;&amp;=&lt;&gt;/]*" title="The following characters are not allowed in the Name field: &quot;&amp;=&lt;&gt;/"
            maxlength=""
            data-ga-category="collections_button"
            data-ga-action="create_collection"
            data-ga-label="non_favorties_collection"
            required
          />
        </div>
        <div class="action-panel-control-wrap existing-collections-controls">
             <label for="collections-action-dialog-add-to-existing" class="usa-label margin-top-0">
                Choose a collection
              </label>
              <select id="collections-action-dialog-add-to-existing"
                      class="usa-select collections-action-add-to-existing"
                      data-ga-category="collections_button"
                      data-ga-action="select_collection"
                      data-ga-label="($('.collections-action-add-to-existing').val() === 'Favorites') ? 'Favorites' : 'non_favorites_collection'">
              </select>
              <div class="collections-retry-load-on-error usa-input-error-message selection-validation-message">
                Unable to load your collection due to an error<br>
                <a href="#">Please try again</a>
              </div>
        </div>
    </fieldset>

    <div class="display-inline-flex">
        <button class="usa-button margin-top-0 action-panel-submit"
            type="submit"
            data-loading-label="Adding..."
            data-pinger-ignore
            data-ga-category="collections_button"
            data-ga-action="click"
            data-ga-label="add">
          Add
        </button>
        <button class="usa-button usa-button--outline margin-top-0 action-panel-cancel"
                aria-label="Close 'Add to Collections' panel"
                ref="linksrc=close_collections_panel"
                data-ga-category="collections_button"
                data-ga-action="click"
                data-ga-label="cancel">
          Cancel
        </button>
    </div>
</form>
    </div>
  </div>
</div>



      </div>
    </div>
